EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
INTRODUCTION 
The California Governor’s Office of Emergency Services (Cal OES), Office of 
Grants Management, Victim Services (VS) Branch presents the annual fiscal and 
programmatic status report to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee for fiscal 
year (FY) 2021-22. This report provides the Legislature, our stakeholders, and 
Subrecipients with a comprehensive understanding of the work being 
accomplished across California through our programs. This report lists each of 
the VS Branch’s programs, Grant Subawards, fund sources, funded amounts, 
budget program/components, and the resulting statistics gathered for 
FY 2021-22 (July 1, 2021, to June 30, 2022) for each VS Branch program. 
In FY 2021-22, the VS Branch administered approximately $453 million in funding, 
including much needed COVID-19 funding, through programs that respond to 
victims’ needs or promote an effective criminal justice system response to 
victims. The following pages provide a snapshot of the activities, allocations, 
and statistical data related to the nearly 90 victim services and criminal justice 
grant programs administered by Cal OES’s VS Branch. The below table is a 
sampling of the total data collected: 
# of

Below are Program Highlights that are included to showcase the outstanding 
efforts of state, local, and community-based victim services and criminal justice 
organizations that Cal OES’s VS Branch funds. The funding allows for prevention, 
intervention, and enforcement components that work toward achieving a safer 
California. The need for these services is on-going – without continued state and 
federal grant funding, many of these exemplary projects would cease to exist. 
PROGRAM HIGHLIGHTS 
AMERICAN RESCUE PLAN UNIT 
The Sacramento Regional Family Justice Center (SRFJC) serves victims of 
domestic violence, elder abuse, human trafficking, sexual assault, and child 
abuse and is a Subrecipient of the Domestic Violence Rescue Plan (RP) 
Program.  The purpose of the RP Program is to ensure the continuity of domestic 
violence services and/or response to COVID-19 for non-shelter supportive 
1 


--- PAGE 6 ---
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
   
  
  
  
  
 
   
      
  
  
     
   
     
  
  
 
  
 
 
  
  
  
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
services and/or prevention activities. SRFJC was able to increase the number of 
case managers to assist victims with services and support the need to move 
from a place of danger and trauma to one of safety and hope. Case managers 
listen to victims’ stories and assist with a variety of services based on their unique 
situations, including but not limited to: crisis intervention, counseling, danger and 
lethally assessment, and court accompaniment during restraining order 
hearings. Victims also have access to SRFJC community partners including 
WEAVE, My Sisters House, Sacramento County District Attorney’s Office, Lao 
Community Development, International Rescue Committee, and SAFE (Special 
Assault Forensic Evaluation) Center. 
Case Study: 
A young mother with four children came to the SRFJC, with nothing but her 
purse, as she fled her home after a violent abusive incident with her husband. 
She wanted to get a restraining order, but it was late and the beginning of a 
three-day holiday weekend. SRFJC contacted the local domestic violence 
agency who provided safe shelter for her and her children until she could come 
back to the SRFJC on Tuesday morning to file a restraining order. 
On Tuesday, one of SRFJC attorneys worked with her to file the temporary 
restraining order while a counselor created a safety plan for her and her 
children and offered continuing counseling and support. Child Protective 
Services helped with resources she did not know were available to her. They 
were able to get into transitional housing where they could stay until she could 
get a permanent restraining order. SRFJC staff worked with her during that 
interim time to help her better understand the cycle of violence and helped her 
get in contact with her family who were unaware of her circumstances. The staff 
also helped prepare her for the court hearing and provided court 
accompaniment. 
SRFJC is proud to report that this client and her children are safe, in permanent 
housing now and living a healthy, productive life. 
CHILDREN’S UNIT 
The Regents of the University of California, Davis CAARE Center provides patient 
care, teaching, research, and prevention initiatives for children and youth 
identified as high-risk of abuse and neglect and is a Subrecipient of the Child 
Abuse Treatment (AT) Program. The AT Program provides funding for 
comprehensive psychotherapy services to children victimized by abuse and/or 
neglect. Psychotherapy services for family members and/or caregivers of child 
victims are also available to help with the treatment of the child. 
Case Study: 
A 12-year-old female was referred to the Regents of the University of California, 
2 


--- PAGE 7 ---
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
  
   
 
 
  
  
 
  
 
   
 
  
        
      
    
  
      
 
  
     
   
 
 
 
  
 
  
   
  
    
 
   
  
 
Davis CAARE Center due to a history of sexual abuse by multiple perpetrators, 
exposure to parental substance abuse, and neglect. The child was removed 
from her mother’s care at age 10 and placed with her biological father who she 
had not had a relationship with since age five. Engagement and building trust 
took extended time due to her complex trauma history and being placed in a 
home with a father, stepmother, and younger male half-siblings she did not 
know. The child’s parents had a hard time understanding how her trauma 
affected her ability to trust and build relationships with them. The child’s primary 
therapist focused on the child’s trauma and a separate therapist provided 
collateral trauma-focused cognitive behavioral therapy (TF-CBT) sessions to the 
parents. After a year, the child was able to complete TF-CBT culminating in a 
successful sharing of her trauma experience. The parents made significant 
progress in understanding how the child’s trauma affected her brain 
development. They began to see her strengths, be more responsive to her 
emotional needs, and provide more consistent validating interactions that 
increased her sense of security in the home. 
LAW ENFORCEMENT & COUNTY VICTIM SERVICES UNIT 
The Sacramento Internet Crimes Against Children (ICAC) Task Force focused 
efforts on promoting the Northern California Regional Training Center’s (NCRTC) 
Forensic, Internet Crimes and Wellness Training to 112 affiliated agencies. NCRTC 
hosted multiple forensic and ICAC trainings, providing 107 affiliated agency 
investigators advanced training, this in turn reduced the caseloads within the 
Task Force. The two-day Introduction to Hi-Tech Crime Training, was revitalized 
providing an introduction to digital evidence investigations to over 68 students. 
NCRTC hosted two Supporting Heroes in Mental Health Foundational Trainings 
(SHIFT), promoting officer wellness to 51 investigators. SHIFT training provides tools 
and resources to cope with the negative effects the investigations can take on 
from the exposure to child sexual abuse materials in the workplace. 
Case Study: 
A cybertip from the National Center for Missing and Exploited Children led to a 
perpetrator that was uploading and downloading child pornography. A search 
warrant was written for the perpetrator’s Google accounts. While viewing the 
entirety of one of the videos in the perpetrator’s possession, a child was seen 
wearing a t-shirt from a local middle school. Detectives went to the middle 
school where the student in the video was identified. Detectives authored a 
search warrant and served him the same day. The perpetrator was detained 
and Mirandized. He admitted to sexually abusing the child and filming the 
incidents. Other videos were found depicting the perpetrator and the juvenile 
neighbor in various states of sexual activity. The perpetrator was arrested on 
multiple counts of sexual abuse of a child and possession and manufacturing 
3 


--- PAGE 8 ---
 
 
   
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
  
 
 
   
  
 
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
  
  
  
child pornography. The US Attorney's Office prosecuted the case. 
According to court documents, the perpetrator sexually abused a 13-year-old 
victim, producing multiple visual depictions of those acts. The perpetrator sent at 
least one video recording of the victim to another person over the internet in 
exchange for different files depicting children. On the date of the arrest, over 
200 videos depicting the sexual abuse of children, some as young as toddlers, 
were found on the perpetrator’s iPhone. 
The perpetrator was sentenced on January 6, 2022, to 24 years and four months 
in prison, to be followed by 20 years of supervised release, for sexual exploitation 
of a child. 
DOMESTIC VIOLENCE UNIT 
The Domestic Violence Unit has continued to expand and develop current 
programs through state & federal funds. The development of these programs will 
continue to create and support programs that provide multifaceted prevention 
and intervention efforts to support victims of Domestic Violence as they rebuild 
their lives. In particular, Cal OES has implemented the Gun Violence Restraining 
Order Outreach (GO) Program to facilitate education and training efforts 
related to gun violence restraining orders (GVRO) and how to petition for one 
through an outreach campaign tailored to domestic violence victims, including 
those most marginalized. 
Cal OES has selected the California Partnership to End Domestic Violence 
(CPEDV) to design and implement a public education campaign to drive public 
awareness regarding GVROs and the petition process. This will include the 
development of a website, digital/social media advertisement, and digital and 
physical GVRO pamphlets for distribution to domestic violence 
groups. Additionally, CPEDV will distribute awarded funds to other community-
based domestic violence organizations to conduct outreach to domestic 
violence victims within the community they serve and ensure marginalized 
communities are reached. 
HOUSING UNIT 
‘Jenna’ is a transitional youth who fled her home because her parents were 
being emotionally abusive. They did not approve of her transgender identity. 
When she left to stay at a motel, the parents cut financial ties and Jenna was 
unable to afford the motel any longer. She did not even have a car to stay in. 
This is when she found WISEPlace where she received shelter, nutritious meals, 
and case management support. WISEPlace collected her documentation and 
helped provide her with a transition age youth emergency housing voucher. 
During her time at WISEPlace, she obtained employment and received 
4 


--- PAGE 9 ---
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
  
 
 
   
 
  
  
 
 
   
 
 
    
 
 
  
transportation assistance to help her get to her job. WISEPlace also assisted her 
with her search for housing and aided her in applying to a nearby unit. She 
moved to a 1-bedroom apartment where WISEPlace supplied her with basic 
housewares so she could feel at home. Jenna continues to receive supportive 
services through a partner housing organization and WISEPlace checks in 
regularly to see how she is doing. 
HUMAN TRAFFICKING & LEGAL ASSISTANCE UNIT 
Last year the Coalition to Abolish Slavery & Trafficking (CAST) served 1,625 
survivors of human trafficking and their family members, took over 2,000 hotline 
calls, and trained over 14,000 professionals on how to identify and respond to 
human trafficking. CAST also took the opportunity to share their expertise on the 
realities of human trafficking: how it happens, to whom, what CAST is doing, and 
what more needs to be done. Additionally, they focus on three issues that make 
people particularly vulnerable to human trafficking: homelessness, 
criminalization, and poor health and wellbeing. 
SEXUAL ASSAULT UNIT 
To address the significant impact of COVID-19 on sexual assault service 
programs, 103 Subrecipients funded through the Rape Crisis (RC) Program, 
Campus Sexual Assault (CT) Program, and the Victim Advocacy in Detention 
Facilities (KA) Program were augmented funds through the Family Violence 
Prevention and Services Act (FVPSA) American Rescue Plan (ARP) COVID-19 
Rape Crisis Centers and Sexual Assault Programs (FVSA) Supplemental Funding 
Program. The purpose of these funds is to assist with the transition to 
virtual/remote services for rape crisis centers, sexual assault programs, tribal 
programs, and culturally specific programs that provided crisis services, support 
services, and assistance to survivors of sexual assault and support the increased 
emergency needs of sexual assault survivors as a result of the COVID-19 public 
health emergency. 
Specifically, these funds helped to prepare for, prevent, and respond to COVID-
19. Funding supported a range of in-scope activities including counseling, 
mobile advocacy, telehealth, teletherapy, peer support, temporary housing, 
rental assistance and nominal relocation expenses, supplies, equipment, and 
software to assist in carrying out virtual services. 
UNDERSERVED VICTIMS UNIT 
The Underserved Victims Unit continues to fund victim assistance service 
programs to various underserved populations throughout California. One of 
those programs is the Innovative Response to Marginalized Victims (KI) Program, 
which supports innovative projects and serves marginalized victims of crime and 
provides many various services to victims of poly-victimization. The KI Program 
5 


--- PAGE 10 ---
 
 
  
  
 
 
  
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
  
 
  
  
 
 
 
 
  
  
 
           
      
         
           
        
       
       
      
       
          
focuses on providing direct services to young males of color, victims of gang 
violence, refugees, low wage workers (janitorial, service industry, etc.), 
transgender, and foster care youth. 
The case study below highlights the efforts provided to promote coordinated 
public/private community efforts within the KI Program to assist Native American 
victims with sexual assault victimization. 
Client contacted law enforcement requesting assistance with getting off a tribal 
reservation and to a local Y.W.C.A. women’s shelter. The client was homeless 
and met a male companion and was living in a tent in a nearby town. The mail 
companion physically assaulted her, and the client reported the attack to the 
county sheriff’s office. After this assault, the client met another male companion, 
and she provided housecleaning services at his home. Shortly after the client 
ended up living with this person in exchange for continual housework and meal 
preparation services. Eventually the exchange for housework services led to 
sexual intercourse. A couple of months later, the client became pregnant, and 
after four months was ready to leave. The client stated she didn’t want to have 
intercourse but felt it was her only option from being homeless. The client 
realized shortly that she was being pimped out by overhearing a conversation 
about collecting money for having intercourse with the client. The client 
confronted the perpetrator and he threatened to take her back to the town 
where she was seen as “Indian Gold” as a Caucasian girl on the reservation 
where she would experience being raped by Indian men and/or killed by Indian 
women. The client was able to get away and received prenatal care at Sutter 
Hospital. The client divulged to the nurse about being the victim of sexual abuse 
and prostitution. The client ended up moving and is currently receiving support 
and referral services from VOICES in Santa Rosa to overcome her victimizations. 
VICTIM WITNESS & PROSECUTION UNIT 
The City of San Diego, District Attorney’s Office is a Subrecipient of the Gun 
Violence Restraining Order (GS) Program. The GS Program supports statewide 
gun violence restraining order training. 
The San Diego City Attorney’s Office (San Diego CAO) is recognized as a state 
and national leader in protecting the public through the use of Gun Violence 
Restraining Orders (GVROs). GVROs are a life-saving tool created by California’s 
“red flag” laws to proactively prevent predictable acts of gun violence before a 
tragedy occurs. The San Diego CAO, in partnership and collaboration with the 
San Diego Police Department, launched its program in December 2017. Since 
then, more than 1,700 firearms have been removed in the City of San Diego 
from individuals who threatened violence against co-workers, classmates, 
intimate partners, family members, the general public, and/or themselves. The 
GVROs have also been utilized to prevent mass casualty events, and as a “gap-
6 


--- PAGE 11 ---
 
 
       
          
  
          
         
         
        
     
       
     
        
           
           
        
   
filler” for pending criminal investigations, criminal cases, and situations where 
firearms have not been seized pursuant to another type of restraining order. 
Helping other agencies to utilize the life-saving mechanism of the GVRO is a 
high priority for the San Diego CAO office. Since July 2021, the San Diego CAO, 
through the implementation of the GS Grant Subaward, has trained over 65 
agencies, including law enforcement and those providing law enforcement 
legal representation throughout California. The agencies trained include county 
and federal partners. As the laws and best-practices evolve, many jurisdictions 
have received multiple trainings. Additionally, attorneys from the San Diego 
CAO routinely offer technical assistance to the agencies they have trained as 
they implement their own GVRO programs. Attorneys at the San Diego CAO 
receive an average of five to ten queries per week from different jurisdictions 
regarding how to handle GVROs. Grant funding has facilitated these trainings, 
and the additional support they provide.

1. Victims of Crime Act (VOCA) Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (Formula 
Grant Program) 
The VOCA Formula Grant Program is authorized by the Victims of Crime Act of 
1984 (34 U.S.C. § 20103 (a) and (b)). 
VOCA authorizes federal financial assistance to states through the VOCA 
Formula Grant Program for the purpose of supporting eligible crime victim 
assistance programs that: 1) respond to the emotional and physical needs 
of victims of crime; 2) help primary and secondary victims of crime to 
stabilize their lives after a victimization; 3) help victims of crime understand 
and participate in the criminal justice system; and 4) provide victims of 
crime with a measure of safety and security. Crime victim is defined as any 
person who has suffered physical, sexual, financial, or emotional harm as a 
result of the commission of a crime. 
2. Services*Training*Officers* Prosecutors (STOP) Violence Against Women (VAW) 
Formula Grant Program 
The STOP VAW Formula Grant Program was authorized in 1994 through the 
Violence Against Women Act(VAWA). VAWA, Public Law 103-322 was 
reauthorized in 2000, 2005 and most recently in 2013. The STOP Formula Grant 
Program is administered by the Office on Violence Against Women, Office of 
Justice Programs, U.S. Department of Justice. 
The STOP Formula Grant Program promotes the development and the 
implementation of effective, victim-centered law enforcement, 
prosecution, and court strategies to address violent crimes against women 
and the development and enhancement of victim services in cases 
involving violent crimes against women. States and communities are 
encouraged to restructure and strengthen the criminal justice system 
response to domestic violence, sexual assault, and stalking, drawing upon 
the experience of all participants in the system. 
3. The Family Violence Prevention and Services/Domestic Violence Shelter and 
Supportive Services (FVPS) Grant 
The FVPS Grant is authorized by 42 U.S.C. § 10401, Family Violence Prevention 
and Services Act. 
The Family Violence Prevention and Services/Domestic Violence Shelter 
and Supportive Services grant, administered through the Administration on 
Children, Youth and Families’(ACYF) Family and Youth Services Bureau 
(FYSB), is designed to assist states in their efforts to support the 
establishment, maintenance, and expansion of programs and projects to: 
11 


--- PAGE 16 ---
 
 
  
 
 
  
  
  
 
 
    
 
  
 
 
   
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
  
 
  
  
  
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
1) prevent incidents of family violence, domestic violence, and dating 
violence; 2) provide immediate shelter, supportive services, and access to 
community-based programs for victims of family violence, domestic 
violence, or dating violence, and their dependents; and 3) provide 
specialized services for children exposed to family violence, domestic 
violence, or dating violence including victims who are members of 
underserved populations (45 CFR § 1370.10(a)). 
4. Children’s Justice Act (CJA) Grants to States Program 
The CJA Grants to State Program is authorized by Section 107(a) of the Child 
Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (P.L. 111-320). 
The purpose of the CJA Grant is to assist States in developing, establishing 
and operating programs designed to improve: (1) the assessment and 
investigation of suspected child abuse and neglect cases, including cases 
of suspected child sexual abuse and exploitation, in a manner that limits 
additional trauma to the child and the child’s family; (2) the assessment 
and investigation of cases of suspected child abuse-related fatalities and 
suspected child neglect-related fatalities; (3) the investigation and 
prosecution of cases of child abuse and neglect, including child sexual 
abuse and exploitation; and (4) the assessment and investigation of cases 
involving children with disabilities or serious health-related problems who 
are suspected victims of child abuse or neglect. 
5. Sexual Assault Services Formula Program (SASP)-specific to the RC Program 
The Sexual Assault Services Formula Program (SASP) is authorized by 34 U.S.C. 
§12511. SASP was created by the Violence Against Women and Department of 
Justice Reauthorization Act of 2005, as amended by the technical amendments 
to that act, and is the first federal funding stream solely dedicated to the 
provision of direct intervention and related assistance for victims of sexual assault. 
The SASP Formula Program directs grant dollars to states and territories to 
assist them in supporting rape crisis centers and other nonprofit, 
nongovernmental organizations or tribal programs that provide core 
services, direct intervention, and related assistance to victims of sexual 
assault. Rape crisis centers and other nonprofit and tribal organizations, 
such as dual programs providing both sexual violence and domestic 
violence intervention services, play a vital role in assisting sexual assault 
victims through the healing process, as well as assisting victims through the 
medical, criminal justice, and social support systems. Funds provided 
through the SASP Formula Program are designed to supplement other 
funding sources directed at addressing sexual assault at the state and 
territorial level. 
12 


--- PAGE 17 ---
 
 
 
  
 
   
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
6. State General Fund (SGF) 
The California State Budget Act authorizes funding, in statute, each fiscal year to 
specific programs. These programs support services for victims including, but not 
limited to, housing for homeless youth, legal resources and information, family 
violence prevention, and training and technical assistance. 
7. State Penalty Fund (SPF) 
The State Penalty Fund was created as a depository for assessments on specified 
fines, penalties, and forfeitures imposed and collected by the courts for criminal 
offenses and used to support victim services programs, amongst others. These 
State funds can be used for match requirements following the provisions of the 
Subrecipient Handbook, Section 6522. 
13

BEST PRACTICE INITIAL CHILD INTERVIEW VIDEO TRAINING PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Best Practice Initial Child Interview Video Training (VQ) 
Program is to educate first responders on how to properly conduct a 
forensically-sound, trauma-informed victim interview during an initial child sexual 
abuse investigation to ensure successful prosecution. 
This is accomplished through the creation of three training videos: a Commission 
on Peace Officer Standards and Training certified video for law enforcement; a 
video specific to Child Protective Services personnel; and one for the broader 
category of first responder-mandated reporters. These training videos are to be 
made available statewide. 
The VQ Program is supported through Children’s Justice Act (CJA) Grant 
Funds. 
Program/Component: 0385.902

BULLYING & VIOLENCE IN SCHOOL ADVOCACY PROGRAM 
The purpose of the Bullying & Violence in School Advocacy (XB) Program is to 
provide counseling, advocacy, and other support services to child/teenagers who 
have been victimized by bullying and/or school violence. 
Required components of the XB Program include counseling, advocacy, and 
support services. Advocacy and support services may include, but are not limited 
to: anonymous tip line; criminal justice advocacy; and crisis intervention, safety 
planning, referrals, and parental/caregiver support to aid in the child/teenager’s 
recovery. 
The XB Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and the federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

CALIFORNIA ADVANCING THE PRISON RAPE ELIMINATION ACT PROGRAM 
The purpose of the California Advancing the Prison Rape Elimination Act (AP) 
Program is to enhance and increase services for victims of sexual assault in state and 
local correctional facilities. The AP Program allows Subrecipients to work with the 
California Department of Corrections and Rehabilitation (CDCR) to renew/secure 
formal partnerships between all CDCR facilities and their local Rape Crisis Centers 
(RCCs). 
The components of the AP Program include, but are not limited to: securing 
operational Memorandums of Understanding between RCCs and CDCR facilities to 
ensure victims have access to a Rape Crisis Counselor for accompaniment/advocacy 
services; providing training and technical assistance to RCCs; and establishing 
Second-Tier Subawards to administer financial support to participating RCCs. 
The AP Program is supported with funds through the Services*Training*Officers* 
Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING ALLOCATED

CALIFORNIA CASA ASSOCIATION TRAINING AND RECRUITMENT 
PROGRAM 
The purpose of the California CASA Association Training and Recruitment (KR) 
Program is to increase services to children in foster care by recruiting Court 
Appointed Special Advocate (CASA) volunteers statewide and to provide the 
required training to CASA volunteers. 
Required components of the KR Program include but are not limited to: 
updating the training curriculum for CASA volunteers; conducting a minimum of 
three statewide trainings; implementing statewide marketing efforts to recruit 
CASA volunteers; and advertising volunteer training opportunities. 
The KR Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) 
– Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and the federal Victims of Crime 
Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

FY 2023-24 Unknown

23 Number of outreach/volunteer recruitment events held. 
The CASA University curriculum is continuously updated, as needed, and 
includes eight training modules. Since its launch, CASA University has been 
adopted by nearly 80% of the local CASA programs. The CASA University 
provides advocates with learning modules, videos, and lesson plans via Zoom. 
The Indian Child Welfare Act (ICWA) curriculum was updated in collaboration 
with the Judicial Council.

CALIFORNIA VICTIM  WITNESS ADVOCATE TRAINING  PROGRAM  |  202 3  
The purpose of the California Victim Witness Advocate Training (TP) Program is to 
design curriculum and provide high-quality, statewide training to Victim Witness 
(VW) Program Advocates, Supervisors, Coordinators, and allied professionals. 
Pursuant to Penal Code §13835.10, the TP Program establishes statewide minimum 
selection and training standards for VW Program Advocates and administrative 
personnel who provide direct victim services. 
Required components of the TP Program include the following trainings: 
40-hour Entry Level Advocate Academy; Advanced Advocate Academy; 
Management/Supervisory Academy; and Train-the-Trainer courses. Upon request, 
the TP Program provides ongoing technical assistance to VW Centers statewide. 
The TP Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

CALIFORNIA’S  CHILDREN’S JUSTICE ACT  EVALUATOR PROGRAM| 2023  
The purpose of the California’s Children’s Justice Act Evaluator (CJ) Program is 
to support identified programs by developing assessments and tools that will be 
used to assist with meeting program goals and identifying recommendations 
that will fill program needs or gaps. 
Required components of the CJ Program include: 
• Hosting the Children Exposed to Domestic Violence (CEDV) database and 
conducting outreach to California law enforcement agencies to encourage 
use of the database; 
• Finalizing the Guidance Document to include best practices and tips for 
rollout, training, and implementation of an Electronic Suspected Child Abuse 
Report System (eSCARS); 
• Providing assistance with the evaluation of CJA-funded training programs; 
and 
• Collaborating with Cal OES and the CJA Task Force. 
The CJ Program is supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice 
Act Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 CJA0 FFY20 $ 100,000 
FY 2022-23 CJA0 FFY19 $ 16,666 
CJA0 FFY20 $ 83,334 
FY 2023-24 Unknown 
2021-22 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS   
Northwest Professional Consortium Research, Inc. compiled information through 
interviews with Subrecipients who received funding through the eSCARS 
Program. The information was used to create and finalize the eSCARS 
Guidance Document that can be used by counties throughout California as a 
reference tool when developing their own eSCARS. The Guidance Document 
includes the steps necessary to develop eSCARS, building on lessons learned by 
the counties who had already developed their own, including how to: convene 
key partners and build support for eSCARS; determine the approach to building 
eSCARS; obtain child welfare data; build and test the system; plan system roll 
out and train staff; implement eSCARS; and ensure sustainability. 
Northwest Professional Consortium Research, Inc. reviewed CJA-funded training 
programs and provided evaluation support and consultation for the 
development of knowledge tests, satisfaction questions, and follow up surveys. 
CJ PROGRAM 


--- PAGE 32 ---
   
 
 
  
  
  
   
 
CALIFORNIA’S CHILDREN’S JUSTICE ACT EVALUATOR PROGRAM| 2023 
Northwest Professional Consortium Research, Inc. assisted with a new CJA-
funded program by developing an evaluation plan and surveys so the 
effectiveness of the program has measurable outcomes.

CAMPUS SEXUAL  ASSAULT  PROGRAM  |  2023  
The purpose of the Campus Sexual Assault (CT) Program is to maintain a 
comprehensive, coordinated, community response that is victim-centered, 
enhances victim safety, provides confidential services to sexual assault victims, 
holds offenders accountable, and includes comprehensive outreach activities 
at institutions of higher education. 
This is accomplished through the development of clear policies and protocols 
on the handling of reports of sexual assault, ongoing trainings on sexual assault 
and sensitivity for all campus staff, and specialized services for victims. A Sexual 
Assault Counselor from a local Cal OES-funded Rape Crisis Center (as defined in 
Evidence Code §1035.2) is located on campus, promoting the availability of the 
services, and providing training to students, Police/Safety Officers, campus 
investigatory personnel, staff, and faculty. 
The CT Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) - Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), federal Family Violence 
Prevention and Services/Sexual Assault/Rape Crisis Services and Supports (FVSA), 
federal Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula 
Grant Program (STOP), and federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance 
Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.154, 0385.161, 0385.451, 0385.452

CHILD  ABDUCTION TRAINING PROGRAM  | 2023  
The purpose of the Child Abduction Training (AC) Program is to collaborate 
with the Child Abduction Task Force to provide statewide training to 
multidisciplinary professionals within the child abduction field. The AC Program 
also manages the Child Abduction Task Force website by keeping it updated 
with training information and relevant resources about child abduction. 
Required components of the AC Program include: 
• Collaborating with the members of the Child Abduction Task Force to 
facilitate Child Abduction Intervention and Resource Training as well to 
facilitate Child Abduction Simulated Exercises; 
• Updating the Child Abduction Website with resources and information for 
professionals who may come into contact with missing or abducted 
children and/or their families; and 
• Surveying participants at the end of each training session to ensure topics 
and information remain relevant; and 
• Providing oversight of the Child Abduction Task Force to include 
appointments and dismissals of Task Force members and managing travel 
arrangements and expenses for travel related to training and meetings. 
The AC Program is supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice 
Act Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 CJA0 FFY19 $ 96,250 
CJA0 FFY20 $ 68,750 
FY 2022-23 CJA0 FFY20 $ 15,000 
CJA0 FFY21 $ 150,000 
FY 2023-24 Unknown 
2021-22 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS    
Virtual and in-person training included the following: 
• The First 24 Hours (formerly named The first 48 Hours) 
• Child Abduction Intervention and Resource training 
• Virtual Child Abduction Intervention and Resource Training 
• Rethinking Runaways Training 
• Resources and Strategies for Investigating the Living Doe 
• Virtual Child Abduction Investigations for Rural Counties

articles written by members of the Child Abduction Task Force.

CHILD ABUSE T REATMENT  PROGRAM  | 2023  
The purpose of the Child Abuse Treatment (AT) Program is to provide 
comprehensive psychotherapy services to children victimized by abuse and/or 
neglect. Psychotherapy services for family members and/or caregivers of child 
victims are also available to help with the treatment of the child. 
Required components of the AT Program include: 
• Providing free comprehensive treatment services to child victims and 
secondary victims to include psychotherapy and/or culturally-centered 
therapy, crisis intervention, group counseling, case management, 
treatment plan meetings with parents/caregivers, criminal justice 
orientation, transportation services to psychotherapy and/or criminal 
justice-related activities, and outreach; 
• Providing services to child victims who do not have access to services due 
to lack of available resources, lack of mental health insurance, and/or non-
eligibility for other programs or services; 
• Prioritizing services to child victims who are underserved or are dependents 
of the court and involved in the child welfare system; and 
• Assisting child victims with California Victim Compensation Board claims. 
The AT Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) 
– Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

CHILD  ADVOCACY CENTER  PROGRAM  | 2023  
The purpose of the Child Advocacy Center (KC) Program is for Child Advocacy 
Centers (CACs) to provide direct victim services to child abuse survivors and 
their families through a multidisciplinary team approach. These services must 
include victim-centered, trauma-informed forensic interviews, advocacy, direct 
or referral to therapy/counseling, and referrals for medical exams. 
California Penal Code §11166.4 states “each county may use a children’s 
advocacy center to implement a coordinated multidisciplinary response, 
pursuant to Section 18961.7 of the Welfare and Institutions Code, to investigate 
reports involving child physical or sexual abuse, exploitation, or maltreatment. A 
county that utilizes a CAC to coordinate its multidisciplinary team shall require 
the CAC to meet standards pursuant to California Penal Code §11166.4, part b.” 
Required components of the KC Program include, but are not limited to: a 
multidisciplinary team consisting of law enforcement, child protection, 
prosecution, mental health, medical, and victim advocacy professionals who 
work together to help children heal from abuse; a dedicated child-focused 
setting designed to provide a safe, comfortable, neutral place where forensic 
interviews and other CAC services may be provided for children and families; 
resource and referral services, and case review and tracking. 
The KC Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime 
Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

CHILD SEXUAL ABUSE TRAINING AND TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 
| 2023 
The purpose of the Child Sexual Abuse Training and Technical Assistance 
(NC/SC) Program is to provide training and technical assistance to service 
providers who work directly with child sexual abuse victims or who handle child 
sexual abuse cases. 
Pursuant to the requirements of Welfare and Institutions code §18275, the NC/SC 
Program funds training centers, one in Northern California (Northern Child Sexual 
Abuse and Technical Assistance Center) and one in Southern California 
(Southern Child Sexual Abuse and Technical Assistance Center). The training 
centers are responsible for developing training programs for interagency teams 
of service providers throughout California, including law enforcement, social 
services, health care providers, and mental health providers. Service providers 
who receive the training will then provide training in their communities. 
Required components of the NC/SC Program include: providing training on 
sexual abuse issues; providing up to four Child Forensic Interview Training classes; 
providing training topics that cover the issues and needs of child sexual abuse 
victims with disabilities; ensuring training is culturally and linguistically competent; 
providing course certification to meet licensing and/or continuing education 
needs of the attending professionals; and using training assessments that 
evaluate the trainers and the effectiveness of the training curriculum. 
The NC/SC Program is supported with the following funds: State General 
Funds (SGF) – Child Abuse Prevention/Training (CSAP), State General 
Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), and federal Victims of 
Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.353, 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

CHILD SEXUAL ABUSE TREATMENT PROGRAM
The purpose of the Child Sexual Abuse Treatment (CS) Program is to provide 
comprehensive services to children under the age of 19 who are victims of 
sexual abuse and/or sexual exploitation. Services may also be provided to the 
child victim’s family members to aid in the child’s recovery. 
Required components of the CS Program include: 
• Crisis intervention; 
• Individual counseling; 
• Referrals to other services; and 
• Follow-up support. 
The CS Program is supported with the following funds: State General 
Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), State Penalty 
Funds (SPF) – Child Sexual Abuse and Exploitation (CSAE), federal Family 
Violence Prevention and Services Act (FVPSA) American Rescue Plan (ARP) 
COVID-19 Rape Crisis Centers and Sexual Assault Programs (FVSA), and federal 
Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.154, 0385.354, 0385.451, 0385.452

COUNTY VICTIM SERVICES PROGRAM
The purpose of the County Victim Services (XC) Program is to assess the existing 
victim service structure, identify needs, and fill gaps in victim services in all 
counties statewide and in the City of Los Angeles. 
Services provided through the XC Program must: provide victim-centered, 
trauma-informed direct victim services to respond to the emotional, 
psychological, and/or physical needs of marginalized crime victims; assist victims 
to understand and participate in the criminal justice system; restore a measure of 
security and safety for the victim; and/or assist victims with compensation claims 
through the California Victim Compensation Board. 
The XC Program is supported with the following funds: State General Fund 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime 
Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

COURT APPOINTED SPECIAL ADVOCATES PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Court Appointed Special Advocates (KS) Program is to fund 
Court Appointed Special Advocate (CASA) Programs in California in order to 
provide more children in foster care with a CASA volunteer. 
CASAs are trained community volunteers appointed by juvenile court judges to 
advocate for children who are placed into the protective custody of the child 
welfare system due to abuse or neglect. CASAs review court facts and 
documents pertaining to the child’s case and submit formal reports to the judge 
at every scheduled hearing/review. The CASA monitors the child’s case to 
assure that the judicial and child welfare systems are moving ahead to secure a 
safe, permanent home for the child, and that court-ordered services are 
provided to the child. 
Required components of the KS Program include: recruiting volunteers in order 
to increase the number of children who receive advocacy services from a 
CASA volunteer; training CASA volunteers pursuant to the Welfare & Institutions 
Code Sections 102(d) and 103(a); and providing oversight, support, and 
supervision to CASA volunteers. 
The KS Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of 
Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452

COURT EDUCATION AND TRAINING  PROGRAM  | 2023 
The purpose of the Court Education and Training (CW) Program is to develop 
and provide trainings, seminars, publications, symposiums, and other programs 
designed to increase the knowledge of court personnel in cases involving 
domestic violence, sexual assault, stalking, and teen dating violence. These 
trainings are offered to both state and tribal court judges and all judicial officers. 
Required components of the CW Program include but are not limited to: 
implementation of a training needs assessment; development and 
implementation of a training plan; curriculum development and ongoing 
revisions to ensure all curriculum provides judicial education specifically in family, 
criminal, juvenile, civil, and probate courts; and development and distribution of 
training materials. 
The CW Program is supported with the following fund: federal Services*Training 
*Officers*Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 STOP FFY21 $ 768,597 
FY 2022-23 STOP FFY22 $ 768,597 
FY 2023-24 Unknown 
2021-22 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
3 Number of updates to existing publications on domestic violence, 
protective orders, stalking, and sexual assault. 
10/216 Number of sexual assault, domestic violence, stalking, dating 
violence, and human trafficking courses for judicial staff/total 
number of attendees. 
2/138 Number of ethics and self-represented litigants in domestic violence 
cases courses provided to judicial officers/total number of 
attendees. 
3/481 Number of multidisciplinary trainings provided to juvenile law, family 
law, and domestic violence stakeholder/total number of attendees. 
1/305 Number of domestic violence-related webinar courses provided to 
court staff, court interpreters, and other domestic violence service 
providers/total number of attendees.

CRISIS RESPONSE TRAINING PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Crisis Response Training (CR) Program is the implementation 
of comprehensive crisis response training and technical assistance to local 
Victim Witness Assistance (VW) Programs and multi-disciplinary teams to 
establish response readiness to mass victimization crimes. 
Required components of the CR Program include: 
• Annual training needs assessment; 
• Curriculum development; 
• Regional and statewide Crisis Response trainings; 
• Train the Trainer trainings; and 
• Mass Victimization (MV) Advocate Roundtable meetings. 
The CR Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of 
Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE AMERICAN RESCUE PLAN PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence American Rescue Plan (RP) Program is to 
ensure the continuity of domestic violence services and/or response to COVID-19 
by providing funding for non-shelter supportive services and/or prevention 
activities. 
Non-shelter supportive services and/or prevention activities provided to the adult 
and youth victims of domestic violence and their dependents include, but are not 
limited to: 
• Individual and group counseling; 
• Peer support groups; 
• Advocacy and case management; 
• Legal advocacy; 
• Services for children; 
• Gift cards for clients for needs related to victimization; 
• Gift cards for clients as incentives for participation in focus groups and/or 
surveys; 
• Transportation–gas cards, Uber, and Lyft vouchers; 
• Outreach activities to increase the awareness of domestic violence; 
• Primary, secondary, and tertiary prevention activities; 
• Safety plan development; and 
• Emergency preparedness efforts. 
The RP Program is supported with funds through the federal Family Violence 
Prevention and Services/Domestic Violence Shelter and Supportive Services Grant 
(FVPSA) – Supplemental Funding American Rescue Plan (ARP) of 2021. 
Program/Component: 0385.150 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE ASSISTANCE PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Assistance (DV) Program is to provide 
comprehensive support, including emergency shelter to victims of domestic 
violence and their children, and local assistance to existing domestic violence 
service providers. The DV Program also provides support for the development 
and establishment of domestic violence services to unserved and underserved 
populations, including, but not limited to, rural areas, non-English speaking 
individuals, persons of color, and various geographical areas with limited access 
to services. 
Pursuant to California Penal Code §§13823.15-13823.16, required components of 
the DV Program include: 
•  Twenty-four-hour crisis hotlines  
•  Counseling for children  
•  Counseling (individual and peer  
group)  
•  Household establishment  
assistance  
•  Business centers  •  Transportation  
•  Emergency  shelter  for victims and  
their children  
•  Criminal justice and social services  
advocacy  
•  Emergency food and clothing  •  Court accompaniment  
•  Emergency response to calls from  
law enforcement  
•  Diversity, equity, and inclusion 
plan  
•  Language access plan  •  Local community service network  
•  Legal assistance with  temporary  
restraining  orders and other  
protective orders  
•  Medical  advocacy and  
emergency response  
The DV Program is supported with the following: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Domestic Violence (DVP0), State General Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime Act 
Supplemental (VCGF), federal Family Violence Prevention and Services 
American Rescue Plan (FVPA) Program, federal Family Violence Prevention and 
Services Grant to States for Domestic Violence Shelters and Support Services 
Program (FVPS), federal Services*Training*Officers *Prosecutors Violence Against 
Women Formula Grant Program (STOP), and federal Victims of Crime Act Victim 
Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
This Program was augmented and extended through September of 2022. 
Program/Component: 0385.151, 0385.161, 0385.451, 0385.452 
DV PROGRAM

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE HOUSING FIRST PROGRAM | 2023 
2The purpose of the Domestic Violence Housing First (XD) Program is to provide 
victims of domestic violence with safe, permanent housing and ongoing 
supportive services tailored to address the individual needs of each victim. 
The XD Program is designed after the Housing First Model, as defined in Welfare 
and Institutions (W&I) Code Section 8255 to address the prevalence of 
homelessness in California. The Housing First Model is an approach to connect 
individuals and families experiencing homelessness quickly and successfully to 
permanent housing without preconditions and barriers to entry, such as sobriety, 
treatment, or service participation requirements. 
Required components of the XD Program include, but are not limited to: housing 
assistance; financial assistance; and supportive services for victims. Organizations 
that receive XD Program funding must also hire one full-time equivalent 
advocate, who has met the requirements of a “Domestic Violence Counselor” as 
described in Evidence Code §1037.1(a)(1) as well as one half-time equivalent 
advocate in the role of Housing Coordinator. 
The XD Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) 
– Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), federal Family Violence Prevention 
Act (FVPA), and Federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant 
Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.151, 0385.451, 0385.452

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE MOBILE HEALTH CARE SERVICES PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Mobile Health Care Services (FO) Program is 
to provide domestic violence victims/survivors and their children access to COVID-
19 testing, vaccines, and primary health care through weekly mobile health care 
visits. 
To ensure all areas of California are reached, Cal OES is utilizing a regional 
approach where one Subrecipient coordinates services for all domestic violence 
shelters and programs within the identified region. 
The FO Program is supported by the following funds: Family Violence Prevention 
and Services Act (FVPSA) American Rescue Plan COVID-19 Testing, Vaccines, and 
Mobile Health Unit Supplemental Funding Program (FVCO). 
Program/Component: 0385.153

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE RESPONSE TEAM PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Domestic Violence Response Team (VA) Program is to support 
agencies in building protocols and practices for a collaborative and community-
coordinated response to domestic violence. The VA Program builds the capacity of 
communities that demonstrate a need for greater collaboration and coordination 
between domestic violence service providers and law enforcement organizations. 
The required components for the VA Program include, but are not limited to: 
establishing law enforcement and advocacy liaisons; improving domestic violence 
protocols; ensuring prompt community response; providing training, tools and 
materials; creating a capacity building plan; co-locating; entering into Operational 
Agreements/Second Tier Subawards with specific agencies (e.g., local law 
enforcement, district attorney’s office[s]), and assisting with California Victim 
Compensation Board Claims. 
The VA Program is supported with federal Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors 
Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

DRUG ENDANGERED CHILDREN  TRAINING  PROGRAM  | 2023  
The purpose of the Drug Endangered Children Training and Advocacy Center (DK) 
Program was to provide training on victim-centered, trauma-informed response to 
children who are at risk or are victims of child abuse and/or neglect and those who 
are exposed to drugs, drug paraphernalia, grow houses, methamphetamine 
laboratories, gang activity, or firearms. 
In-person and web-based training was provided to first responders, investigators, 
prosecutors, and social service providers. Additionally, the Subrecipient provided 
ongoing technical assistance to first responders and social services providers. 
The DK Program was supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice 
Act Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902

EARLY IDENTIFICATION OF COMMERCIALLY SEXUALLY EXPLOITED CHILDREN 
TRAINING PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Early Identification of Commercially Sexually Exploited Children 
Training (EI) Program was to provide comprehensive training to K-12 educators and 
students on the early identification and intervention of sexual exploitation of children 
and youth (CSEC), and other severe forms of trafficking. 
Required components of the training topics for the EI Program included, but were not 
limited to: definition, background, and overview of human trafficking; understanding 
the indicators and signs for identifying children and youth at risk or victims of 
exploitation and trafficking; culturally-competent and trauma-informed care; and 
protocols for reporting and intervention. The training and tools include separate 
modules designed specific to educators and to students. 
The EI Program was supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice Act 
Grants to States Program (CJA). 
Program/Component: 0385.902 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

ELDER ABUSE PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Elder Abuse (XE) Program is to continue to enhance the 
safety of elder and dependent adult survivors of crime by providing direct 
services to survivors and bridging the gap between elder justice service 
providers and victim services providers. 
This is accomplished by the following: 
• Funding an Elder Abuse Victim Specialist solely dedicated to serving elder 
and dependent adult survivors; 
• Providing/coordinating direct services for elder and dependent adult survivors; 
• Expanding the communities’ capacity to service elder and dependent 
adult survivors; 
• Ensuring that elder and dependent adult survivors have access to the 
criminal justice system; and 
• Continuing to provide outreach awareness programs for survivors, 
mandated reporters of elder and dependent adult abuse, and others who 
come into contact with these survivors. 
The XE Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of 
Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

ELECTRONIC SUSPECTED CHILD ABUSE REPORTING SYSTEM PROGRAM| 
2023 
The purpose of the Electronic Suspected Child Abuse Reporting System (ES) 
Program is to improve the system of sharing information for suspected child 
abuse cases by establishing an electronic Suspected Child Abuse Report (SCAR) 
system that will be used by district attorney offices, local law enforcement, and 
county child welfare services departments. 
Required components of the ES Program include: 
• Convening key stakeholders, including the district attorney office, child 
welfare services department, and local law enforcement agencies; 
• Identifying the approach for building the electronic suspected child abuse 
reporting system (eSCARS); 
• Building and testing the new eSCARS ; 
• Developing a plan for rollout and sustainability; and 
• Transitioning the current SCAR process to eSCARS. 
The ES Program is supported with funds through the federal Children's Justice Act 
Grants to States Program (CJA0). 
Program/Component: 0385.902 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 CJA0 FFY20 $ 130,877 
CJA0 FFY21 $ 469,123 
FY 2022-23 CJA0 FFY21 $ 162,592 
CJA0 FFY22 $ 437,408 
FY 2023-24 Unknown 
2021-22 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
This was the first year of funding for the Subrecipients. Each Subrecipient has 
identified the approach they are going to use in developing their eSCARS and 
are in the planning phase of the development. 
Fresno County, Santa Barbara County, and Yolo County were previous 
Subrecipients who received funds through the ES Program. Each county has 
developed their eSCARS and has implemented the process of using eSCARS.

EMERGENCY RESPONSE TO INTERPERSONAL VIOLENCE PROGRAM 
|2023 
The Emergency Response to Interpersonal Violence (CO) Program was 
implemented as a first step in combating the impacts of the COVID-19 
pandemic on victim service providers to ensure the immediate safety of victims 
of interpersonal violence (including domestic violence, sexual assault, human 
trafficking, and child abuse victims). 
Three statewide coalitions received the funding to distribute to service providers 
to support the additional needs associated with providing safety and services for 
victims during the pandemic and to comply with social distancing guidelines. 
The CO Program was supported with the following funds: federal Victims of Crime 
Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA) and Family Violence and 
Prevention Services/Domestic Violence Shelter and Supportive Services 
Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act (FVPC). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.925 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

FY 2022-23 Program ended August 15, 2021

EMERGING FAMILY JUSTICE CENTER PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Emerging Family Justice Center (FE) Program is to support 
emerging Family Justice Centers (FJCs) to meet the criteria defined in California 
Penal Code §13750. 
Emerging FJCs must ensure coordinated services are provided to victims to 
enhance the safety of victims and service providers who participate in affiliated 
survivor-centered support or advocacy. 
The FE Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Family Justice 
Center (FJC0). 
Program/Component: 0385.921 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

Subrecipients were non-operational during this performance period.

Program began April 1, 2022, so there are no statistics to report.

EQUALITY IN PREVENTION AND SERVICES FOR DOMESTIC ABUSE 
PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Equality in Prevention and Services for Domestic Abuse (GL) 
Program is to maintain and expand domestic violence services for the lesbian, gay, 
bisexual, transgender, and questioning (LGBTQ) communities in order to increase 
access to culturally appropriate domestic violence education, prevention, 
outreach, and services for these unserved/underserved communities. 
Pursuant to California Penal Code §13823.17 the GL Program provides: 
• Crisis intervention (Initial and Ongoing); 
• Legal advocacy; 
• Participation in domestic violence multidisciplinary teams (including, but not 
limited to, law enforcement, medical treatment facilities, and other service 
providers); 
• 24-hour crisis hotline; 
• Business Centers; 
• Counseling services (Individual and Peer Group); 
• Legal assistance (Temporary Restraining Orders/Other Protective Orders and 
Custody Disputes); 
• Criminal Justice advocacy; 
• Social Service advocacy; 
• Educational workshops and publications; 
• Community resources and referrals; 
• Emergency shelter access; and 
• Household establishment assistance. 
The GL Program is supported with the following federal funds: Family Violence 
Prevention and Services Grant to States for Domestic Violence Shelters and Support 
Services Program (FVPS), Family Violence Prevention Services CARES Act (FVPC), the 
Family Violence Prevention Services Act American Rescue Plan (FVPA) and the 
following state fund: State General Funds (SGF) – Equality in Prevention and Services 
for Domestic Abuse (EPSD). 
Program/Component: 0385.151, 0385.906 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

FAMILY JUSTICE CENTER PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Family Justice Center (FJ) Program is to support fully 
operational, multiagency, multidisciplinary Family Justice Centers (FJCs) 
pursuant to California Penal Code §13750-13751. 
An FJC is a service center where public and private agencies assign staff 
members on a full or part-time basis to provide services to victims of domestic 
violence, sexual assault, elder abuse, dependent adult abuse, or human 
trafficking. FJCs provide centralized access to services and support for victims 
and their children, reduce the number of places victims must go for help, and 
reduce the number of times victims must reiterate their experience of abuse. 
The FJ Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Family Justice 
Center (FJC0). 
Program/Component: 0385.921 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING

FARMWORKER WOMEN’S SEXUAL ASSAULT & DOMESTIC VIOLENCE 
PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Farmworker Women’s Sexual Assault & Domestic Violence 
(FW) Program is to develop and implement activities aimed at addressing and 
combating the issues of sexual assault and domestic violence (SA/DV) among 
farmworker women who are low-income, monolingual or bilingual Spanish-
speaking. 
This is accomplished through the following: 
• Providing statewide outreach and education on SA/DV among 
monolingual, bilingual Spanish-speaking, and low-income farmworker 
women; 
• Developing and incorporating two additional Chapters to expand services 
across California; 
• Providing training to agencies addressing SA/DV in the farmworker 
community; 
• Developing and continuously updating outreach materials focusing on the 
special needs of farmworker women; 
• Disseminating information to Cal OES-funded Rape Crisis and Domestic 
Violence Subrecipients regarding the unique service provision needs of 
farmworker women; 
• Linking farmworker SA/DV victims to available resources; 
• Coordinating and collaborating with Rape Crisis Centers, Domestic 
Violence Shelters and Victim/Witness Programs in all Chapter areas; and 
• Coordinating and hosting community awareness and educational events, 
addressing sexual assault and domestic violence, for farmworker women 
from all Chapters. 
• Involving indigenous women in SA/DV awareness efforts and conducting 
educational meetings in indigenous Mexican farmworker communities. 
The FW Program is funded with the federal Services*Training*Officers*Prosecutors 
Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.161

GUN VIOLENCE RESTRAINING ORDER OUTREACH PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Gun Violence Restraining Order Outreach (GO) Program is to 
inform domestic violence victims of the availability of gun violence restraining 
orders and how to petition for one through an outreach campaign tailored to 
domestic violence victims, including those most marginalized. The GO Program will 
assist with the development of the materials, create additional materials as 
needed, implement the campaign, and subaward funds to local domestic 
violence groups to conduct target outreach in their communities with emphasis on 
the most marginalized victims of domestic violence. 
The GO Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds 
(SGF) – Gun Violence (DVST). 
Program/Component: 0385.105

HOMELESS YOUTH AND EXPLOITATION PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Homeless Youth and Exploitation (HX) Program is to help 
homeless youth exit street life. Services provided under the HX Program include: 
access to food; shelter/housing; counseling; outreach services; screening/providing 
for basic health needs; linkage to services offered by public and private agencies; 
long-term stabilization planning; and follow-up services. In addition, the HX Program 
provides specialized services for youth experiencing sexual exploitation. 
The HX Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Homeless Youth (HY00), State Penalty Fund (SPF) – Child Sexual Abuse & Exploitation 
(HY05), State General Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF), and 
federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.351, 0385.451, 0385.452

HOMELESS YOUTH EMERGENCY SERVICES AND HOUSING PROGRAM | 
2023 
The purpose of the Homeless Youth Emergency Services and Housing (YE) 
Program is to establish or expand access to a range of housing options and 
provide crisis intervention and stabilization services to homeless youth so the 
immediate crises they face can be resolved and they can focus on their future. 
During this performance period, YE Program Subrecipients were strongly 
encouraged to work collaboratively with local colleges to provide support, 
services, and housing options to homeless students. 
Pursuant to California Welfare and Institutions Code §13701, the YE Program 
provides: 
• Food/meals and access to shelter; 
• Counseling to address the immediate emotional crisis or problem; 
• Outreach services such as drop-in facilities; 
• Screening and providing for basic health care needs; 
• Referrals to other services offered by public and private agencies, such as 
employment services, law enforcement agencies, social services, schools, 
mental health and healthcare providers, and local victim/witness assistance 
centers; and 
• Long-term stabilization planning, and follow-up services, to ensure that family 
reunification or placement outside the family is stable. 
The YE Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Homeless Youth 
(HY00). 
Program/Component: 0385.351 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

there has been no additional funding allocated.

HUMAN TRAFFICKING ADVOCATE PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Human Trafficking Advocate (HA) Program is to provide 
funding to Victim/Witness Assistance Centers, as defined in the California Penal 
Code § 13835.2, for dedicated, specialized staff and supports to identify and 
provide comprehensive services to victims of human trafficking, including sex 
and labor trafficking. 
Required components of the HA Program include, but are not limited to: 
• Crisis intervention; 
• Emergency assistance; 
• Resource and referral assistance; 
• Direct counseling/therapy; 
• Assistance with California Victim Compensation Board claims; 
• Property return; 
• Orientation to the criminal justice system; 
• Court escort; 
• Outreach to criminal justice agencies; 
• Community outreach and awareness; 
• Case status/disposition; 
• Notification of family/friends; 
• Employer notification/intervention; 
• Restitution; and 
• Linkage to other services offered by public and private agencies. 
Organizations that receive HA funding must employ at least one half-time 
Victim/Witness Advocate dedicated to human trafficking victims. 
The HA Program is supported with: State General Funds (SGF) – Victims of Crime 
Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act Victim Assistance 
Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 SGF-VCGF FY21 $ 574,206 
VOCA FFY20 $ 1,045,258

VOCA FFY21 $ 539,822

HUMAN TRAFFICKING VICTIM ASSISTANCE PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Human Trafficking Victim Assistance (HV) Program is to help 
human trafficking victims recover from the trauma they experienced and assist 
with reintegration into society through the provision of comprehensive safety 
and supportive services using a trauma-informed, victim-centered approach. 
Required components of the HV Program include, but are not limited to: access 
to a 24-hour crisis hotline for victims; emergency shelter and temporary housing 
assistance; emergency food and clothing; and counseling. Organizations that 
receive funding through the HV Program must also ensure staff working with 
human trafficking victims qualify as Human Trafficking Caseworkers as described 
in Evidence Code §1038.2 and are trained according to the minimum 
requirements therein. 
Since fiscal year 2019-20, many HV Program Subrecipients shifted focus to better 
identify and serve victims of labor trafficking as well as sex trafficking. Examples 
have included additional training for victim service advocates and partner 
organizations (such as hospital/health clinic staff), co-locating human trafficking 
caseworkers in hospitals, clinics, and family justice centers, and the 
development of local multi-disciplinary teams with expertise in addressing the 
needs of labor trafficking victims. Beginning in fiscal year 2020-21, HV Program 
Subrecipients have worked to integrate remote access to their services, such as 
through text and mobile apps, to preserve accessibility to services for victims 
impacted by stay-at-home orders issued due to COVID-19. 
The HV Program is supported through State General Funds (SGF) – Human 
Trafficking Victim Assistance (HTVA). 
Program/Component: 0385.912 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

IMPROVING OUTCOMES FOR CHILD  AND  YOUTH VICTIMS OF  HUMAN  
TRAFFICKING  PROGRAM  |  2023  
The Improving Outcomes for Child and Youth Victims of Human Trafficking 
(IO) Program brings together the Alameda County District Attorney’s 
Office (ACDAO), Cal OES, and the California Department of Social Services to 
improve outcomes for child and youth victims of human trafficking. 
The goals of the IO Program included: engaging in detailed planning with a 
multidisciplinary team of state leaders and stakeholders; developing and conducting 
a comprehensive multiday, multidisciplinary training to identify best practices and 
improve coordinated local responses; and evaluating existing and new program 
models to determine the impact of outcomes for child and youth victims of human 
trafficking, and identifying effective strategies. 
The IO Program was supported with funds through the federal Improving Outcomes 
for Child & Youth Victims of Human Trafficking Program (IOCV). Funds were allocated 
in FY 2017-18. This was a one-time funded program that ended August 31, 2021. 
Program/Component: 0385.920 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 Program ended August 31, 2021* 
FY 2022-23 N/A 
FY 2023-24 Unknown 
*The final two months of the program occurred in FY 2021-22. 
2021-22 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
The IO Program is not a direct services program, rather, the goals and objectives 
focused on supporting regional and statewide strategies for collaboration and 
coordination of services to improve outcomes for child and youth victims of human 
trafficking. Activities for the IO Program included: 
• Developing evaluation and data collection instruments (through subcontractor 
WestEd) to support third party evaluation of services and supports implemented 
by the Improving Outcomes for Transition Age Youth Victims of Human Trafficking 
Pilot (IP) Program Subrecipients; 
• Providing technical assistance through capacity-building meetings (through 
subcontractor WestEd) to the IP Program Subrecipients. During this period, 
WestEd provided 37 technical assistance sessions to IP Program Subrecipients; 
• Planning and logistical coordination for the first of two collaborative learning 
exchanges between the IP Program Subrecipients (through subcontractor 
IO PROGRAM 


--- PAGE 160 ---
    
    
 
   
  
     
  
  
 
      
   
   
    
      
 
  
 
    
 
   
   
 
 
IMPROVING OUTCOMES FOR CHILD AND YOUTH VICTIMS OF HUMAN 
TRAFFICKING PROGRAM | 2023 
WestEd). The first, which focused on establishing and working with multi-
disciplinary teams, occurred in August 2020 via video/teleconference due to 
COVID-19 pandemic restrictions. The second focused on client services, occurred 
in March 2021, again, via video/teleconference; and 
• Planning and hosting the comprehensive, multiday, multidisciplinary training, 
which occurred June 7–8, 2021. ACDAO gathered a diverse group of participants 
for the virtual Multi-day Conference. The forum of speakers represented a 
spectrum of knowledge and experience in Combatting Human Trafficking. 
Speakers for the event included representatives throughout the State from district 
attorney’s offices, victim witness, probation, tribes, service providers, and most 
inspiringly, people with lived experience. The conference provided an 
opportunity for conversation and considerations, as well as a call to action, for 
expansion of the commission and task forces in California. Speakers gave 
presentations on topics relevant to human trafficking in their communities and 
disciplines, and then led conversations in breakout rooms. The IP Program 
Subrecipients presented on their experience with the implementation of their 
programs and lessons learned.

INNOVATIVE RESPONSE TO MARGINALIZED VICTIMS PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Innovative Response to Marginalized Victims (KI) Program is to 
support innovative services for marginalized victims of crime. The KI Program 
supports the following survivor groups: victims of gang violence or young male 
victims of color, refugee victims, small agencies never before funded by 
Cal OES, low wage workers (janitorial, service industry, etc.), transgender, and 
foster care youth. 
Required components of the KI Program include, but are not limited to: providing 
direct victim services to respond to the emotional, psychological, or physical 
needs of marginalized crime victims; restoring a measure of safety and security for 
the survivor; assisting survivors to understand and participate in the criminal justice 
system; and assisting survivors to stabilize their lives after victimization. 
The KI Program is supported with the following funds: State General Funds (SGF) – 
Victims of Crime Act Supplemental (VCGF) and federal Victims of Crime Act 
Victim Assistance Formula Grant Program (VOCA). 
Program/Component: 0385.451, 0385.452 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

INTERNET CRIMES AGAINST CHILDREN PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Internet Crimes Against Children (IC) Program is to ensure 
that local law enforcement agencies are adequately equipped to investigate 
cybercrimes that target youth; provide educational programs aimed at law 
enforcement officers, teachers, parents, and children; serve as a forensic 
resource to law enforcement agencies within each service area; pursue the 
prosecution of cases at the local, state, and federal level; and participate in 
nationally-coordinated investigative efforts. 
Required components include: conduct proactive investigations into 
technology crimes perpetrated against children and youth; serve as a forensic 
resource to affiliate agencies within the Task Force’s geographical service area; 
assist and enhance prosecutorial efforts at the local, state, and federal level; 
participate in law enforcement and community outreach activities to provide 
prevention, education and training; formalize law enforcement partnerships 
through signed Operational Agreements; participate in nationally coordinated 
investigations; and, assist with arrests for technology crimes perpetrated against 
children and youth. 
The IC Program is supported with State General Funds (SGF) – Internet Crimes 
Against Children (ICAC). 
Program/Component: 0385.908 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED

FY 2022-23 SGF-ICAC FY22 $ 4,750,000

INTIMATE PARTNER VIOLENCE PREVENTION PROGRAM | 2023 
The purpose of the Intimate Partner Violence Prevention (FD) Program is to 
prevent intimate partner violence, including teen dating violence, prioritize 
underserved populations within communities, and build the capacity of local 
organizations to do this work. 
Required components of the FD Program include: 
• Commitment of one full-time equivalent Prevention Staff to accomplish 
the goals of the FD Program; 
• Implementation of a Prevention Program; 
• Develop an evaluation plan through the assistance of technical 
assistance providers to determine how short-term, intermediate, and long 
term outcomes and impact will be measured; 
• Modeling web-based training curriculum; 
• Resources for Voluntary Participation in Services. 
The FD Program is supported with the following: federal Family Violence 
Prevention and Services Grant to States for Domestic Violence Shelters and 
Support Services Program (FVPS), and federal Services*Training*Officers* 
Prosecutors Violence Against Women Formula Grant Program (STOP). 
Program/Component: 0385.152, 0385.161 
FISCAL YEAR FUND SOURCE(S) FUNDING 
ALLOCATED 
FY 2021-22 FVPS FFY 21 $ 850,005 
STOP FFY21 $ 499,995 
FY 2022-23 FVPS FFY22 $ 850,005 
STOP FFY22 $ 499,995 
FY 2023-24 Unknown 
2021-22 PERFORMANCE STATISTICS 
Personnel – Prevention Staff 
This reporting period is establishing the first year of the FD Program and captured 
initial data and recap of the statistical outcomes in narrative form. Subrecipients 
encountered very few delays in the hiring/retention of prevention staff as 
identified in the FD Grant Subaward. Data was consistent that there were no 
delays due to high turnover, insufficient salary, insufficient benefits, or heavy 
workload issues. If staff shortages or delays were mentioned the common theme 
was relating to COVID-19. 
FD PROGRAM

INTIMATE PARTNER VIOLENCE PREVENTION PROGRAM | 2023 
Project Implementation 
The reported data from all nine FD Program Subrecipients suggest that there 
were minimal issues with project implementation. Each project implementation 
plan outlined their marginalized population of focus, development of prevention 
curriculum and its dissemination, and numerous prevention events that were 
conducted and numbered in the hundreds across the State. 
Many aspects of the focused populations’ center around vulnerable immigrant 
youth and their families, high risk youth, youth engaged in the foster care 
system, unhoused youth, and youth involved with the justice system. Targeting 
these marginalized groups with prevention strategies towards teen dating 
violence and other intimate partner violence by teaching healthy relationship 
skills to prevent sexual violence, domestic violence, and teen dating violence 
and promote social norms that protect against violence. Other offerings 
centered on self-care and empowerment for this audience as well. The 
dissemination of information and implementation of strategies to this audience 
are mostly through outreach efforts with local school districts and other 
education-based community groups and planned community events. 
FD PROGRAM
